01 Concept of Health and Disease |
|
ONCEPT OF HEALTH icM |
In a world of continuous change, new concepts are bound to emerge based on new patterns of thought, |
‘Health has developed 8radually asa concept from an individual concern to a world wide social goal, and it holds
within itself the whole quality of life; Various concepts of health are:
{) Biomedical Concept |
. Traditionally, health has been viewed as an "absence of disease", and if one was free from disease, then the
Person was considered healthy; this concept known as "Biomedical Concept'; and it has the basis in "germ
theory of disease".
e The criticism that is levelled against biomedical concept is that it has minimized the role of environmental,
social, psychological and cultural determinants of health.
° Development in medical and social sciences led to the conclusion that biomedical concept of health was
inadequate. |
(ii) Ecological Concept
e The ecologists put forward an attractive hypothesis which viewed health as a dynamic equilibrium between
man and his environment; and disease a maladjustment of the human organism to environment.
2 Dubos defined health saying "Health implies the relative absence of pain and discomfort and a continuous
adaptation and adjustment to environment to ensure optimal function.
id The ecological concept raises two issues viz imperfect man and imperfect environment. |
° History argues strongly that improvement in human adaptation to natural environment can lead to longer
life expectancies and a better quality of life, even in the absence of modern health delivery services.
(iii) Psychosocial Concept
aye Contemporary development in social sciences revealed that health is not only a biomedical |
but one which is influenced by social, psychological, cultural, economical and political factors of the People|
concerned; these factors must be taken into consideration in defining and measuring health; thus health 's|
both a biological and social phenomenon. |
|
fiv) Holistic Concept =
. It is a synthesis of all the above concepts; its recognizes the strength of social, economic, political and |
environmental influences on health.
ial fe The holistic approach implies that all sectors of society have an effect on healthy, in peer! eT
P| , imal husbandry, food, industry, education, housing, public work, communications and other sector.

DEFINITION OF HEALTH AND DIMENSIONS OF HEALTH
I d not merely an
e: WHO definition: "Health is a state of complete physical, mental and social well-being an ,
absence of disease or infirmity. sfie di ioral
© Dimensions of health: Health is multidimensional. The WHO definition envisages a oe aed fi
~ the physical, the mental and the social; many more may be cited, viz, spiritual, emotiona
political dimensions.
Physical Dimension
© Thestate of physical health implies the notion of "perfect functioning” of the body. ag
- in, bri ir
© Thesigns of physical health in an individual are: "a good complexion, a clean skin, bright sey a ss
with a body well clothed with firm flesh, not too fat, a sweet breath, a good appetite, soun sl Fe
activity of bowels, and bladder and smooth, easy, coordinated bodily movements; All the organs o! i
are of ideal size and function "normally"; all the special senses are intact, ne pulse rate, blood Pree and)
exercise tolerance capacity are all within the range of "normality" for the individual's age and gender.
) ~ Tools to evaluate of physical health r
(i) Self-assessment
(ii) Standardized questionnaires
(ii) Clinical examination
(iv) Anthropometric measurements
(v) Nutrition and dietary assessment |
] (vi) Biochemical and laboratory investigations.
Mental Dimensions
° Mental health has been defined as "a state of balance between the individual and the surrounding world, a
state of harmony between oneself and others, a coexistence between the realities of the self and that of other
people and that of the environment’.

e Psychological factors can induce all kinds of illness, not simply mental one, they include conditions such as
essential hypertensions, peptic ulcers and bronchial asthma; some major mental illness such as depression
and schizophrenia have a biological component. |

* Psychologists have mentioned the following characteristics as attributes of a mentally healthy person
(i) Free from internal conflicts; he is not at war with himself.
(ii) Well adapted; he accepts criticism and is not easily upset.
(iii) He searches for identity.
(iv) He has good self-control-balance rationally and emotionally
(W) He faces problems and tries to solve them intelligently ie., coping with stress and anxiety.
| (vi) He has a strong sense of self-esteem.
Social Dimension
© It implies harmony and integration within individual, between each individual and other members of
society and between individuals and the world in which they live.
° It has been defined as the “quantity and quality of an individual's interpersonal ties and the extent of
involvement with the community".
2 Social dimension of health includes the levels of social skills one possesses, social functioning and the ability
to see oneself as a member of a beers ye, cml

QUALITY OF LIFE
* WHO defines it as "the condition of life resulting from the combination of the effects of the complete range of
factors such as those determining health, happiness, education, social and intellectual attainments, freedom of
action, justice and freedom of expression".
© Arecent definition of quality of life by WHO is "the product of the interplay between social, health, economic,
and environmental conditions which effect human and social development. |
“4 There are various indices to measure the quality of life
() Physical quality of life index (PQLI)
(i) Human development index (HDI)
(iii) Human poverty index (HP!)
(iv) Gender - related development index (GDI)
(v) Gender empowerment measure (GEM) |

MMI each of them; the resulting PQLI is thus scaled from 0 to 100,
India has a PQLI value of 65.

Spectrum of Health
e Health and disease lie along a continuum and there is no single cut-off point. .
od The lowest point on the health-disease spectrum is death and the highest point correspond to the WHO.
definition of positive health.
° Health fluctuates within a range of optimum well-being to various levels of dysfunction, including the state
of total dysfunction, namely the death.
e The transition from optimum health to ill-health is often gradual and where one state ends and the other
begins is a matter of judgement.
Positive health
Better health
Freedom from sickness
e "The health sickness spectrum"
Unrecognized sickness
Mild sickness
Severe sickness
Death

(vii) Case Fatality Rate (CFR) }
It measures the risk of person dying from a certain disease within a given time period.
© — Itis calculated as number of deaths from a specific disease during a specific time period divided by
number of cases of the disease during the same time period, usually expressed as per 100.

Disease Se
"A condition in which body health is impaired, a departure from a state of health, an alternation of th
human body interrupting the performances of vital function". 1
"A condition of the body or some part or organ of the body in which its functions are disrupted or deranged
"A maladjustment of the human organism to the environment". ;
Miness #
"It refers not only to the presence of a specific disease, but also to the individuals perceptions and behavio ur
in response to the disease, as well as the impact of that diseases on the psychosocial environment". a
"It is a subjective state of the person who feels aware of not being well". a
Sickness
"It is a state of social dysfunction, i.e., a role that the individual assumes when ill (sickness role).
Control p
The term "Disease Control" describes (ongoing) operations aimed at reducing: |
(i) The incidence of disease. ;
(ii) | The duration of disease, and consequently the risk of transmission. |
(iii) The effect of infection, including both the physical and psychosocial complications. |
(iv) The financial burden to the community. |
Elimination
| It is described as interruption of transmission of disease in a localized geographic area; it is a local]
phenomenon.
. Diseases eliminated from India are guinea worm, leprosy, yaws, polio, maternal and neonatal tetanus.
| Eradication
| Eradication literally means to "tear out by roots"; Eradication of disease implies termination of all transmission
of infection by extermination of the infections agent; It is all or none phenomenon.
|® Smallpox is the only disease that has been eradicated globally. 4
|Concept of Causation q
Epidemiological Triad icM 1.3)
e The agent is the cause of disease; the host is an
| organism, usually a human or an animal, that harbours
| the disease; the environment is those surrounding and
conditions external to the human or animal that cause
| or allow disease transmission; and time accounts for
| incubation periods, life expectancy of the host or the Hest Factors
pathogen, and duration of the course of illness or
| condition.
\@. Agent of infectious diseases include bacteria, viruses, parasite, fungi, and molds; with regard to non-
| infectious disease, disability, injury or death, agents can include chemical from dietary foods, tobacco smoke,
solvents, radiation or heat, nutritional deficiencies or substance such as poison. g
2 The level of immunity, genetic makeup, level of exposure, state of health and overall fitness of the host can
determine the effect a disease organism will have on it. a
Multifactorial Causation |
© After the doctrine of a one to one relationship between cause and disease had been shown to be unjustifiable|_
even for microbial disease like tuberculosis, leprosy, the theory of multifactorial causation was given by|
paper cotter of munich (concept that disease is due to multiple factors); this theory de-emphasizes the germ
-|_-_ theory (or single cause idea). i

Web of Causation
This model was given by MacMahon and Pugh; this model is basically for the study of non-communicable

disease, where the disease agent is often not recognized, but the disease is an outcome of the interaction of

‘multiple factors; the "web of causation" considers all the predisposing risk factors of any type and their complex

interrelationship with each other.

* The web of causation does not imply that the disease cannot be controlled unless all the multiple causes
or chains of causation or at least a number of them are appropriately controlled or removed; sometimes
removal or elimination of just only one link or chain may be sufficient to control disease, provided that link
is sufficiently important in the pathogenetic process.

[NATURAL HISTORY OF DISEASE
* Diseases results fromacomplexinteraction between man, an agent (or cause of disease) and the environment;
| it signifies the way in which a disease evolves over time from the earliest stage of its pre-pathogenesis pI
to its termination as recovery, disability or death, in the absence of treatment oF prevention.
‘al The natural history of disease is best established by cohort studies and other epidemiological studies such as
cross-sectional and retrospective studies.
© Natural history of disease has two phases
(i) Pre-pathogenesis (ie., the process in the environment)
(ii) Pathogenesis (i.e, the process in man).
(i) Pre-pathogenesis Phase
© This refers to the period preliminary to the onset of disease in man; the disease agent has not yet
entered man, but the factors which favour its interaction with the human host are already existing in
the environment.
© This situation is frequently referred to as "man in the midst of disease” or"man exposed to the risk
of disease”.
© The mere presence of agent, host and favourable environmental factors in the pre-pathogenesis period)
| is not sufficient to start the disease in man; an interaction of these three factors is required to initiate the
disease process in man. Z
© Thepre-pathogenesis may be modified by intervention measures such as health promotion and
protection (primary prevention). ‘
(ii) Pathogenesis Phase
@ The pathogenesis phase begins with the entry of the disease “agent” in the susceptible human host)
the further events in the pathogenesis phase are clear-cut in infectious disease i.e., the disease
multiplies and induces tissue and physiological changes, the disease progress through a period of
incubation and later through early and late pathogenesis.
Final outcome may be recovery, disability or death.
© Host may become clinical case, sub-clinical or carrier.
|b screening and secondary prevention (arly diagnosis and treatment) are applied in early pathogenesis
phase; tertiary prevention (Disability limitation and rehabilitation) is applied in late pathogenesis
| phases.

ICEBERG OF DISEASE
* According to this concept, disease in a community may be compared with an iceberg.
Floating tip = What physician sees in the community (clinical cases).
Large submerged portion = The hidden mass of disease (latent, in-apparent, presymptomatic and
undiagnosed cases and carriers in the community)
Water line = Represent demarcation between apparent & inapparent disease
° in some diseases like hypertension, diabetes, anemia, malnutrition, mental illness etc, the unknown morbidity
(ie., the submerged portion of ice berg) far exceeds the known morbidity.
. Epidemiologist is concerned with a "hidden portion of an iceberg" whereas clinician is concerned with "tip
of the iceberg".
© Screening is done for a hidden portion of iceberg whereas diagnosis is made from tip of the iceberg.
e Iceberg phenomenon of disease is not shown by rabies, tetanus, measles, and rubella.

Surveillance
° It has been defined as "the continuous scrutiny of the factors that determine the occurrence and spread of
diseases and other conditions of ill health which are pertinent of effective control’.
Uses
(i) Toestimate the prevalence of health problem in a community.
| (i) To study the natural history of a disease.
| (iii) To detect the disease epidemics.
(iv) To generate the hypotheses in research.
| (v) To facilitate emergency planning.
. Surveillance is considered the best weapon to avert epidemics.
© Surveillance can be conducted at the following levels,
1 (i) Individual surveillance |
(ii) Local population surveillance |
(iii) National population surveillance |
(iv) International surveillance |
Types of Surveillance |
(i) Active Surveillance : In this, health functionaries or any designated persons visit the community and collect |
data on predefined indicators; It is used when there is an indication that something unusual is occurring or!
disease is highly contagious, fatal or in the process of elimination or eradication. |
Example: Malaria (done fortnightly) |
Leprosy (endemic) |
Tuberculosis |

(ii) Passive Surveillance : Criteria are established for reporting diseases, risk factors or health-related event;|
Health practitioners are notified of the requirements and they report events as they come to their attention;
| this is the more common type of surveillance.
| (iil) Sentinel Surveillance: No routine notification system can identify all cases of infection or disease; A method
| for identifying the missing cases and thereby supplementing the notified cases is required; this is known as
| "sentinel surveillance’.
| © — We do extrapolation analysis or mathematical modelling with the data gathered from sentinel sites to
| arrive at an estimated no. of total cases.
| © Example: HIV, Hep-B and Hep-C
| Outdoor air quality monitoring i
| Water quality monitoring
lw) Behaviour Surveillance: It is defined as ongoing systematic collection, analysis, and interpretation of
behavioural data relevant to understanding trends in the transmission of HIV and STIs.
i) Nutritional Surveillance: It include routine collection and compilation of data to know about the details of

LEVELS OF PREVENTION AND MODES OF INTERVENTIONS
|Levels of Prevention
|e Levels of prevention are defined by considering the point of time when interventions are applied for halting
| occurrence or progression of disease or its risk factors; there are four levels of prevention,
| (i) Primordial prevention
| (ii) Primary prevention
| (iii) Secondary prevention
| (iv) Tertiary prevention
|
\(i) Primordial Prevention
| © The prevention of the initiation or progress of risk factors in a region or population groups where it has
| | not yet appeared is primordial prevention; primordial prevention is primary prevention in the purest
| sense.
| © The main intervention in primordial prevention is through individual and mass education.
© Best level of prevention for non-communicable disease.
| wy Primary Prevention
| © Itcan be defined as "action taken prior to the onset of disease, which removes the possibility that a
{ disease will ever occur"; It signifies intervention in the pre-pathogenesis phase of a disease or health
problem or other departure from health.
Wearing personal protective devices (mask or gloves) for prevention of acquiring infection while
r handling patients are method of primary prevention
ns é A i


===== PAGE 17 / 400 =====

© There are two interventions for primary prevention. d
bi () Health promotion (Ex : Health education, Nutritional interventions etc)
(ii) Specific protection (Ex : Vaccines, Contraceptions)
| * The WHO has recommended the following approaches for the primary prevention of chronic disease
where the risk factors are established
| (i) Population (mass) strategy
(ii) High-risk strategy
“[fii) Secondary Prevention
E * _ Itcan be defined as action which halts the progress of a disease at its incipient stage and prevents
4 * The specific interventions are early diagnosis (eg: Screening tests, case finding programmes) and
adequate treatments.
© The drawback of secondary prevention is that the patient has already been subject to mental anguish,
physical pain; and the community to loss of productivity.
iv) Tertiary Prevention
© It is defined as “all measures available to reduce or limit impairments and disabilities minimize
t suffering caused by existing departures from good health and to promote the patient's adjustment to
irremediable conditions".
© — The overall goal of tertiary prevention is to improve quality of life by limiting or delayed complication
reducing disability, restoring function, and providing means for rehabilitation if needed.
© Modes of intervention include disability limitation and rehabilitation. |
Modes of Intervention |
"Intervention" can be defined as any attempt to intervene or interrupt the usual sequence in the development
‘of disease in man. They are,
()) Health promotion
(ii) Specific protection
(iii) Early diagnosis and treatment
(iv) Disability limitation
(v) Rehabilitation
(i) Health Promotion
It is "the process of enabling people to increase control over, and to improve health’; The well known’
interventions in this area are,
4 (a) Health Education
{A large number of disease could be prevented with little or no medical intervention if people were
adequately informed about them and if they were encourage to take necessary precautions in time,
{b) Environmental Modification
‘Acomprehensive approach to health promotion requires environmental modification, such as provision
‘of safe water; installation of sanitary latrines; control of insects and rodents; improvement of housing,
| ete.
(c) Nutritional interventions
‘These comprise food distribution and nutrition improvement of vulnerable groups; child feeding
programmes; food fortification; nutrition education, etc.
(d) Lifestyle and Behavioural Changes
Certain lifestyle and behavioural factors like regular physical activity, health diet, sleep, disciplined
life, ability to manage and coping challenges of life through meditation and yoga are certain behaviors
important for preventing non-communicable disease.

| To avoid disease altogether is the ideal but this is possible only in a limited number of cases; the following]

| @_ Immunization
| (0) Use of specific nutrients

| w
(a Early Diagnosis and Treatment
© A expert committee defined early detection of health impairment as "the detection of disturbances of
homeostatic and compensatory mechanism while biochemical, morphological and functional changes
are still reversible”.
Early detection and treatment are the main interventions of disease control.
| © The earlier a disease is diagnosed and treated, the better it is from the point of view of prognosis
| preventing the occurrence of further case (secondary cases) or any long-term disability
| © It is important in reducing the high morbidity and mortality in certain disease such as essential|
hypertension, cancer cervix and breast cancer; For many others such as tuberculosis, leprosy and STD,
| early diagnosis and treatment are the only effective mode of intervention.
joo Disability Limitation
| © The objective of this intervention is to prevent or halt the transition of the disease process from|
impairment to handicap; the sequence of events leading to disability and handicap are,
| © Impairment is defined as "any loss or abnormality of psychological, physiological or anatomical
structure or function".
| Examples: Loss of foot, defective vision or mental retardation.
| * Disability is defined as "any restriction or lack of ability to perform an activity in the manner or within
| the range considered normal for a human being"; Because of an impairment the affected person may be
| unable to carry out certain activities considered normal for his age, sex etc.
| * The term Handicap is defined as "a disadvantage for a given individual, resulting from an impairment)
| ora disability, that limits or prevents the fulfilment of a role that is normal (depending on age, sex and|
| social and cultural factors) for that individual.
* Disability Prevention
| Itrelates to all the levels of prevention,
(a) Reducing the occurring of impairment, viz immunization against polio (primary prevention).
| (b) Disability limitation by appropriate treatment (secondary prevention).
| (©)_ Preventing the transition of disability into handicap (tertiary preventing).

q
(v) Rehabilitation ——_—_———
It has been defined as "the combined and coordinated use of medical, social; educational and vocational
measures for training and retraining the individual to the highest possible level of functional ability".
Medical rehabilitation - Restoration of function
Vocational rehabilitation - Restoration of the capacity to earn a livelihood
Social rehabilitation - Restoration of family and social relationship
Psychological rehabilitation - Restoration of personal dignity and confidence.
ICD-11
- _ Itis an abbreviation for the international statistical classification of disease and related health problem.
= ICDis revised every 10 years.
- __ Itclassifies diseases according to certain characteristics of disease or injuries such as,
(i) Part of body affected
(ii) By aetiologic agent
(iii) Kind of disturbance of function produced by disease or injury.
(iv) Kind of morbid change produced by disease or injury.
The international classification of disease 11" revision has been adopted by the world health assembly in
2019 and it will come into effect from 1* Jan 2022.
Advantages Over ICD -10
Reflects current medical terminology and information needs.
Functions in digital and electronic health record (EHR) environments. |
More comprehensive for use in broader clinical settings and enables greater international comparability,
with no country specific versions.
ICD-11 Linkage to Other Data Standards |
- International classification of health interventions. |
International classification of functioning, disability and health.
-  SNOMED-CT.
FUNCTIONS OF A PHYSICIAN |
A physician is a person who, having been regularly admitted to a medical school, duly recognized in the
country in which itis located, has successfully completed the prescribed courses of studies in medicine and has
acquired the requisite qualification to be legally licensed to practise medicine (comprising prevention, diagnosis,
treatment and rehabilitation) using independent judgement to promote community and individual health.
The functions of a doctor (physician) may be summarized as follows,
(a) The care of the individual |
A physician must be able to assess the state of health of the individual.
(b) The care of community |
The physician is the leader of the "health team’.
(©) The physician as a teacher
The term "doctor" by derivation means to teach.

PREVALENCE

* Defined as “the total number of ll individuals who have an attribute or disease at a particular time (or

in time or midway through the period’. |

of time (Period Prevalence), |

'* Prevalence can be determined from cross-sectional study |

'© Any systematic error in an
‘ny spaematiceror in an epidemiological study, occuring during data collection, compilation analysis
je Predominantly biases are of 3 types |
(@) Subject bias Error introduced by study subject

Recall bia.
(Investigator bias: Error introduced by investigator
Example : Selection bias |
(ii) Analyzer bias: Error introduced by analyzer
‘+ Hawthorne effect (Attention bias): Study subjects may systematically alter their behaviour when they know
they are being observed. » ™ * ny
‘+ _Berkesonian bias (Admission rate bas): Bias due to hospital case and control being systematically diferent
from each other. " == 7
* _Neyman bias (prevalence incidence bias): Bias due to missing of fatal case, mild/silent cases and cases of
short duration of episode from the study.
Memory /Recal bias.
‘+ Bias can be minimized by blinding |
Confounding
Je Any factor associated with both exposure and outcome, and has an independent effet in causation of
outcome.
J+ ttisfound unequally distributed between study and control groups
+ IRis associated with both exposure and outcome
"+ Hasan independent effec in causation of outcome (thus i i risk factor itself) |
© Confounding can be controlled by Randomization, Restriction, matching, stratification and statistical
modeling

‘Assessment
. The final step is assessment of the outcome of the trial in terms of
(a) Positive Results : That is, benefits of the experimental measures such as reduced incidence or severity|
of the disease, cost to the health service or other appropriate.
tb) evetive Results : That is, severity and frequency of side effects and complications, if any, including,
of RCT
|» Forevaluating new drugs and other treatments of disease.
je For evaluating newer tests of medical care technology. |
‘To assess new programs for screening and early detection.
© For evaluation of effectiveness and efficiency of health services.
; of Randomized Control Trial |
1) Clinical Trial |
q © Drug trials trials of surgical procedures etc; unit of study is "patient* suffering from a given disease.
(i) Field trial (Preventive Trial)
© Units of study are healthy individuals; the trials is undertaken in respect of a preventive procedure as
1a vaccine or personal protective measure, etc use of injectable polio vaccine. |
(ii) Community Intervention Trial
‘© This types of trials are good in knowing the effect of environmental variables or health educational
‘measures. eg: health education provided to people of one village regarding hand hygiene practices
and the other village the health education is not provided and study the outcome in terms of decreased
episodes ofilnss
(iv) Evaluation of Health Services
© Same as that of community intervention trial but one component of cost effectiveness is added.
Example : to reduce the incidence of diarthoea, whether the ORS packets should be distributed or
health education regarding the hygiene practices should be provided to the mother
(v) Ricks Factor Trials
© Attype of preventive trial is the trial of risk factors in which the investigator intervene to interrupt the
‘usual sequence inthe development of disease for those individuals who haversk factors for developing
the disease, often this involves risk factor modification.
(vi) Cessation Experiment

Evaluation of Screening Programmes
New screening programmes should be introduced ony after proper evaluation
‘Rondomized Controlled Triols: Ideally evaluation should be done by a randomized controlled trial in which
one group (randomly selected) receives the screening test, and a control which receives no such test
I) tncsetrted Tas: Tese trials are use to se if people with disease detected through srening appear to
live longer after diagnosis and treatment than patients who were not screened.
Example : Cervical cancer screening indicated that deaths from disease could be reduced if every woman
was examined periodically
‘Other methods: Other methods like case control studies and comparison in trends between areas with
different degrees of screening coverage
Tests in Series and Porollel
| screening ests used in series: A populations subjected to one screening st followed by a2 screening tt;
screening ests applied on those individuals only who test positive on 1*srening ts.
Screening test used in parallel: A population s subjected to two or more screning tests tthe same time
‘each ofthe individuals is subjected to both (or all) screening test.

Virus Excretion
> HAVis excreted in the feces for about 2 weeks before the onset of jaundice and for upto 2 weeks there after.
Host Factors
'+ Infection with HAV is more frequent among children than in adults; infection tends to be mild in children|
and severe among adults,
'+ Equal in both sexes.
+ Immunity after attack probably lass for life second attacks have been reported in about percent of patients
Factors:
'+ ~ Poor sanitation, poor personal hygiene, unsafe water and overcrowding greatly influence the spread of the’
disease.
Mode of Transmission
1+ Feecol oral Route : most common route of transmission: it may occur by direct (person to person) contactor
indirectly by way of contaminated water, fod or milk.
parenteral Route : It is by blood and blood products or by skin penetration through contaminated needles;
this may occur during the stage of viraemia.
Sexual Transmission : It may occur mainly among homosexual men because by oral anal contact.
Period
10t0 50 days (usually 14-28 days)
Ciel Spectrum
‘Symptoms are non specific and ranging from mild to severe.
The common presentations include fever, malas, loss of appetite, diarrhoea, nausea and abdominal pain.
Patent may report passing of paleo clay coloured stool, dark coloured urine, and jaundice.

HEPATITIS-B
+ Hepatitis B (formerly know as "Serum hepatitis") is an acute systemic infection with major pathology in the
liver, caused by hepatitis B virus.
+ Hepatitis B is a viral infection that attacks the liver and can cause inflammation of liver resulting in both,
acute and chronic disease.
Epidemiological Determinates
Agent Factors
‘Agent
© Hepatitis B virus is a complex 42 nm double shelled DNA virus, originally known as the dane particle.
‘© HBV occurs in three morphological forms in the serum ofa patient
(i) Small sphericle particle (22:nm) (i) Tubules of varying length and diameter
(ii) Dane particle (infectious)
Reservoir of infection
© Humanis the only reservoir of HBV; the persistent carrier state has been defined as the presence of HBS Ag|
(with or without concurrent HBe Ag) for more than 6 months
Infected Materials
‘© Blood (main source of infection) and body secretions like saliva, vaginal secretions and semen of infected
person.
Resistance
© Virus is quite stable and capable of surviving atleast 7 days on environmental surfaces.
‘© Itcanbe readily destroyed by sodium hypochlorite and autoclave for 30-60 minutes.
‘Period of Communicability
© Itis usually several months (occasionally years in chronic carriers) or until disappearance of HBs Ag and.
appearance of surface antibody.
Host Factors
‘Ave
| = Outcome of HBV infection depend upon age dependent.
+ Newborn are highly prone to develop chronic HBV infection.
+ Adults show comparatively very low probability of development of chronic HBV infections.

Features
The severity of cholera is dependent on the rapidity and duration of fluid loss; A typical case of cholera
‘shows three stages
fe) Stage of Evacuation
| * The onset is abrupt with profuse, painless, watery diarrhoea followed by vomiting,
| * The patient may pass as many as 40 stools in a day.
~ * Patient passes clear fluid with flakes of mucus called rice watery stools.
‘Stage of Collapse
* Signs and symptoms are due to loss of large volume of isotonic fluid
© The classical signs are sunken eyes, hollow cheeks, scaphoid abdomen, sub normal temperature |
7 TRESS Rus and fect absent pulse unecordabe boo pressure las of kn east shalow
Miu gulch respiration, decreased output of urine, patient becomes retles and complains of intense
thirst and cramps in legs and abdomen.
* Death may occur this stage, due to dehydration and acidosis resulting from diarrhoea
I Stage of Recovery ;
* The patient beings to show signs of clinical improvement; Blood pressure begins rise, temperature|
tetums to normal and urine output is re-established
: ‘ sly within 27 days
Symptoms subside spontancously wit

SYNDROME
Dengue viruses are arboviruses (flavivirus) capable of infecting human, and causing disease
These infections may be
(Asymptomatic
©) Classical dengue fever
(iil) Dengue hemorrhagic fever (DHF)
() Dengue shock syndrome (055)
Dengue fever is a self limiting disease and represents the majority of cases of dengue infection.
jical Determinates
Factors:
Dengue viruses belong to group B arboviruses belonging to genus flavivius of family Flaviviridae, a singe
| stranded virus.
‘There are four Virus Seo types
@ DENVA (
@ DENV2
(i) DENVS
(iv) DENV-4
Infection with any one serotype confer lifelong immunity to that virus serotype
When a person has had classic dengue (e, infection by one serotype), a second infection later by another
serotype increases the likelihood of suffering from DHF/DSS.
‘Ades egypt and Actes alopctus are the two most important vectors of dengue. oa
eeghly domesticated, strongly anhropopilic nervous feeder (ies more than one
Ae Ey i cord cs meds ortho et fh compeon of
the gonotropic cycle).
of Disease

‘of Severe Malaria
__ Severe malaria is characterized by one or more of the following features

'* Impaired consciousness/coma * Metabolic acidosis.

* Repeated generalized convulsions © Shock

© Renal Failure ‘© Temperature > 106°F

* Jaundice ‘© Hyper Parasitemia.

‘Black water fever is characterized by marked intravascular haemolysis with haemoglobinuria; patient with

G6PD deficiency may develop it after therapy with primaquine.

_ Treatment of severe and complicated falciparum malaria

() Artesunate : 24 mg/kg iv or im, followed by 2.4 mg/kg after 12 and 24 hrs, and then once daily for!
7 days; switchover to 3 days oral ACT in between whenever the patient can take and tolerate oral

(i) Arte Mether :3.2 mg/kg im on the 1st day followed by 1.6 mg/kg daily for 7 days; switch over to3 days|
‘oral ACT in between whenever the patient is able to take oral medication.

(iil) Quinine : 20 mg/kg (loading dose) (iv infusion or divided im injection) followed by maintenance dose
of 10 mg/kg 8 hourly; infusion rate should not exceed 5 mg/kg per hour; switch over to oral quinine
10mg/kg 8 hourly with doxycycline 100 mg or clindamycin to complete the 7 day course.

Environment

Infected Aedes is the source of infection.

© Cancer may be regarded as a group of diseases characterized by an:

be successful removed.

Sereoning for Cancer Cervix
Je Screening for cervical cancer has become an accepted clinical practice.
J+ The prolonged early phase of cancer in situ can be detected by the pap smear
J+ Current policy suggests that all women should have a pap test (Cervical smear) at beginning of sexual
activity, and then every 3 years thereafter.
Je Screening for cancer cervix using pap smear requires excessive resources in terms of laboratories, equipment
and trained personnels.
+90) Visual inspection based screening tests such as VIA with magnification (VIAM), and visual inspection post
application of Lugol's iodine (VILI) are some of the alternative screening tests.
+The present strategy is to screen women using visual inspection after application of freshly prepared 5%
‘acetic acid solution (5 mi of glacial acetic acid mixed with 95 ml distilled water).

_ Stop the burning process by removing clothing and irrigating the burns.
Extinguish flames by allowing the person to rol on the ground, ot by applying blanket, or by using

} Inchemical bush, remove or dilute the chemical agent by irigating with large volume of water.

care.
+ Donot apply paste, oil, haldi (turmeric) or raw cotton to the burn.
Do not apply ice because it deepens the injury.
Do not open blisters until topical antimicrobials can be applied, by a health care provider.
Avoid application of topical medication until the patient has been placed under appropriate medical
care.

countries

+ Non - modifiable risk factors of coronary heart diseases - age, sex, Family history, Genetic factors, race
and ethnicity
* Modifiable risk factors of coronary heart diseases - Smoking, raised blood pressure, elevated serum|
cholesterol, diabetes, obesity, sedentary lifestyle and stress

* The most common cancer among females worldwide is Breast cancer

MATERNAL MORTALITY RATIO (MMR)

'* Maternal deaths expressed as per 100,000 live births, where a ‘maternal death’ is defined as death of a women
while pregnant or during delivery or within 42 days (6 weeks) of termination of pregnancy, irrespective al
duration or site of pregnancy, from any cause related to or aggravated by the pregnancy or its management
but not from accidental or incidental causes

© MMRisa ratio (Maternal mortality ratio is a misnomer; MMR is not a rate)

‘No.of maternal deaths (due to complication of pregnancy, childbirth or within
MMR in India : 97 per 100,000 live births (2024)

(@ Haemorshage (38%) (i) Other conditions (34%) (ii) Sepsis

(iv) Abortion (v) Obstructed labour. (vi) Hypertensive disorder.

Objectives of School Health Service
The objectives of the programme of a school health service are as follows :
\() | The promotion of positive health.
\(i) The prevention of disease.
(ii) Early diagnosis, treatment and follow up of defects.
iv) Awakening health consciousness in children.
\¥) The provision of healthful environment.
Aspects of School Health Service

Health Appraisal

It consists of periodic medical examinations and observation of children by class teacher.

(a) Periodic: Medical Examination : School health committee in India recommended medical examination of
children at the time of entry and there after every 4 years.
© Initial examination should be through and include a careful history, physical examination, tests for

vision, hearing & speech, clinical examination for nutritional deficiency, intestinal parasitosis ete.

é (b) School Personnel : Medical examination should be given to teachers and other school personnel as they

ry form part of the ‘environment to which the child is exposed.

"| (0) Dally Morning inspection: The teacher carry out the “daly inspection’, as he is familiar with children
i and can detect changes in the child's appearance or behaviour that suggest illness or improper growth
i & development.

Remedial Measures and Follow Up

© Medical examination should be followed by appropriate treatment & follow up.

* Special clinics should be conducted exclusively for school children at the primary health centres in
the rural areas, and in one of the selected schools or dispensaries for a group of about 5,000 children in the
urban area.

CIAL WELFARE PROGRAMMES
es social welfare programmes for women and children in India may be broadly categorized under
() Programmes for the welfare of women.
(i) Programmes for the welfare of children.
- Gii) Composite programmes (both for women and children)
(iv) Schemes for the maladjusted groups.
~ (v) Schemes for the physically handicapped persons.
| (vi), Programmes for the welfare of backward classes.
Child welfare covers the entire spectrum of needs of children who by reason of handicap - Social, economic,
"physical or mental - are unable to avail of services provided by the community.
"Child welfare services in their various facets are preventive, promotive, developmental and rehabilitatives
in nature. .
"Important child welfare agencies in India are :
(Indian council for child welfare (ICCN)
" (ii) Central south welfare Board.
(ii) Kasturba Gandhi Memorial trust
(iv) Indian Red Cross Society.
The following are some of their activities
(Day care services : This is for children of working mother; it is mainly for infants and toddlers,
{Gi) Holiday Homes: For children inthe age groups 12 to 16 years the children spend their holidays in a
useful manner. isk
fi) Recreation Facilities: These comprise organization of lay centres, public parks children's libraries, Bal
Bhavan's, children’s films, etc.

123. The correct answer is (a) UNICEF
+ Baby Friendly Hospital Initiative is created and promoted by WHO and UNICEF a
+ Principles of BFHI: ey :
= Comply fully withthe International Code of Marketing of Breast-milk Substitutes and rely
World Health Assembly resolutions.
- Have a written infant feeding policy that is routinely ‘communicated to staff and parents,
Establish ongoing monitoring and data-management systems.
= Ensure that staff have sufficient knowledge, competence and skills to support breast-feeding,
= Discuss the importance and management of breast-feeding with pregnant women and the
families.
Facilitate immediate and uninterrupted skin-to-skin contact and support mothers to initiate
feeding as soon as possible after birth. ‘
= Support mothers to initiate and maintain breast-feeding and manage common difficulties.
= Do not provide breast-fed newborns any food or fluids other than breast milk, unless medi
indicated.
= Enable mothers and their infants to remain together and to practice rooming-in 24 hours a day,
~ Support mothers to recognize and respond to their infants’ cues for feeding.
~ Counsel mothers on the use and risks of feeding bottles, teats and pacifiers. 4
